I’m not really sure how to answer that straight. I mean, I don’t sit around thinking, “Do I like this or not?” I just kind of do things because they’re on the schedule.

Yeah… I guess I do. I don’t know if I’d call it sad every minute, exactly. It’s more like this heavy, low feeling that’s just kind of sitting there most days.

I still get through my routes, and I still show up for my volunteer shifts and all that. Like yesterday I was driving past this stretch of road where there’s always construction—same orange barrels for what feels like forever—and I caught myself thinking, “Well, here I am again.” Not really upset, just… flat. Like I’m going through the motions instead of really being in my own life.

So, yeah. Sad, low… whatever the right word is. It’s been there a long time.

Yeah… I mean, guilty might not be the exact word, but I do get down on myself a lot.

I keep thinking about my dad and the kind of man he was. He always seemed like he knew what needed doing, just handled things. Even little stuff—like if a neighbor’s fence was leaning, somehow he’d end up over there with a toolbox. I’ll be driving around and see a guy about his age used to be, out fixing something in the yard, and my mind just goes there.

Nobody’s really telling me I’m failing or anything. It’s more me telling myself. Like I should be further along than I am, or I should be handling all this better by now. So… yeah, I guess there’s guilt in that. Or shame. Something like that.

I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.

It’s not something I’ve sat down and planned out, if that’s what you mean. Like I’m there, but not really in my own life, if that makes sense.\n\nI kept thinking it was just being tired, or getting older, you know, long days on the road can do that to a person. But it’s more like this heaviness that doesn’t really lift much.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying the things you used to enjoy, like volunteering?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I still go, so I guess there’s something to it. Volunteering keeps me busy, and I know the folks there, so it’s not like I hate it. But “enjoy” is a funny word, you know? Like when I was younger, I used to get really into things—fixing up my old pickup, going out after work, that kind of stuff.\n\nWith volunteering, I mostly just show up and do what needs doing. Sometimes it feels good to be useful, but I can’t say I’ve really thought about whether I’m enjoying it. It’s more just… part of the routine now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that straight. I mean, I don’t sit around thinking, “Do I like this or not?” I just kind of do things because they’re on the schedule.\n\nLike with volunteering, I show up, help out, talk to whoever’s there. Sometimes it’s fine. I wouldn’t say I’m excited about it, but I don’t know if that means I’ve lost interest or if I’m just… older and more tired. Back when I was younger, sure, I’d get more fired up about stuff, but life was different then. Work takes a lot out of you, especially driving all day—you see the same roads enough times and everything starts to feel kind of the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience pleasure in any activities?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe sometimes? It’s hard for me to tell what counts as pleasure anymore. Like, if I stop for coffee on my route and the weather’s decent, I can think, “Well, this isn’t bad.” But that’s different than feeling really good about something.\n\nI still do things, you know. I keep busy. And sometimes just being around people at the volunteer place is alright. There’s this one guy there who always tells the same story about his fishing trip, and I’ve heard it probably ten times, but I still let him tell it. Kind of funny how people do that.\n\nBut, yeah… I don’t know. I’m not sure I’d say I feel pleasure the way you mean it. I haven’t really paid close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I really look forward to much in the way people mean, like counting down to it or anything. I still have things I do, same as always. Work, volunteering, errands… sometimes on my route I’ll think about stopping at this one diner if I’m nearby, because they’ve got decent pie, though half the time I forget or I’m running behind.\n\nSo, I mean, maybe little things? But I wouldn’t say I’m excited about them. It’s more like… if they happen, fine. If they don’t, I don’t get too worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. I don’t know if I’d call it sad every minute, exactly. It’s more like this heavy, low feeling that’s just kind of sitting there most days.\n\nI still get through my routes, and I still show up for my volunteer shifts and all that. Like yesterday I was driving past this stretch of road where there’s always construction—same orange barrels for what feels like forever—and I caught myself thinking, “Well, here I am again.” Not really upset, just… flat. Like I’m going through the motions instead of really being in my own life.\n\nSo, yeah. Sad, low… whatever the right word is. It’s been there a long time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about three years now. It kind of started after my father died. Or that’s when I noticed it, anyway.\n\nFunny how time gets away from you after something like that. I still remember clearing out his garage—he kept every little screw and coffee can full of bolts, like he was going to build a whole new house out of leftovers. I thought I was doing alright at the time, just staying busy, but somewhere in there this heaviness just settled in and never really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big way that I’ve noticed. I mean, my belt fits about the same, most of my work pants are the same ones I’ve had for years—though one pair shrank funny in the wash, but that was probably the dryer, not me.\n\nI don’t really weigh myself unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something. Day to day, I haven’t looked in the mirror and thought, “Oh, I’ve really changed.” So… no significant change that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, guilty might not be the exact word, but I do get down on myself a lot.\n\nI keep thinking about my dad and the kind of man he was. He always seemed like he knew what needed doing, just handled things. Even little stuff—like if a neighbor’s fence was leaning, somehow he’d end up over there with a toolbox. I’ll be driving around and see a guy about his age used to be, out fixing something in the yard, and my mind just goes there.\n\nNobody’s really telling me I’m failing or anything. It’s more me telling myself. Like I should be further along than I am, or I should be handling all this better by now. So… yeah, I guess there’s guilt in that. Or shame. Something like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nIt’s not something I’ve sat down and planned out, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes my mind goes to dark places, especially when I’m driving long stretches and there’s nothing but highway and radio static, and you start thinking too much. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve… thought about ending my life exactly.\n\nI guess I mostly think about being tired. Like wanting everything to let up for a while. But no, I don’t have some plan or anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’m not sure. I mean, I see it on the news, like anyone does, and sometimes a song comes on from when I was younger and I’ll think about people who aren’t around anymore… but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.

I don’t sit around thinking about death all day or anything. Sometimes it kind of passes through my mind, usually when I’m driving past an accident or one of those roadside crosses—you see a lot of odd things from the road, honestly, people leave flowers and little stuffed animals and then months later they’re all faded from the sun.

But… often? I don’t think so. Not in a way I’ve really kept track of.

Work takes a lot out of you, especially driving all day—you see the same roads enough times and everything starts to feel kind of the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience pleasure in any activities?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe sometimes? It’s hard for me to tell what counts as pleasure anymore. Like, if I stop for coffee on my route and the weather’s decent, I can think, “Well, this isn’t bad.” But that’s different than feeling really good about something.\n\nI still do things, you know. I keep busy. And sometimes just being around people at the volunteer place is alright. There’s this one guy there who always tells the same story about his fishing trip, and I’ve heard it probably ten times, but I still let him tell it. Kind of funny how people do that.\n\nBut, yeah… I don’t know. I’m not sure I’d say I feel pleasure the way you mean it. I haven’t really paid close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I really look forward to much in the way people mean, like counting down to it or anything. I still have things I do, same as always. Work, volunteering, errands… sometimes on my route I’ll think about stopping at this one diner if I’m nearby, because they’ve got decent pie, though half the time I forget or I’m running behind.\n\nSo, I mean, maybe little things? But I wouldn’t say I’m excited about them. It’s more like… if they happen, fine. If they don’t, I don’t get too worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. I don’t know if I’d call it sad every minute, exactly. It’s more like this heavy, low feeling that’s just kind of sitting there most days.\n\nI still get through my routes, and I still show up for my volunteer shifts and all that. Like yesterday I was driving past this stretch of road where there’s always construction—same orange barrels for what feels like forever—and I caught myself thinking, “Well, here I am again.” Not really upset, just… flat. Like I’m going through the motions instead of really being in my own life.\n\nSo, yeah. Sad, low… whatever the right word is. It’s been there a long time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about three years now. It kind of started after my father died. Or that’s when I noticed it, anyway.\n\nFunny how time gets away from you after something like that. I still remember clearing out his garage—he kept every little screw and coffee can full of bolts, like he was going to build a whole new house out of leftovers. I thought I was doing alright at the time, just staying busy, but somewhere in there this heaviness just settled in and never really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big way that I’ve noticed. I mean, my belt fits about the same, most of my work pants are the same ones I’ve had for years—though one pair shrank funny in the wash, but that was probably the dryer, not me.\n\nI don’t really weigh myself unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something. Day to day, I haven’t looked in the mirror and thought, “Oh, I’ve really changed.” So… no significant change that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, guilty might not be the exact word, but I do get down on myself a lot.\n\nI keep thinking about my dad and the kind of man he was. He always seemed like he knew what needed doing, just handled things. Even little stuff—like if a neighbor’s fence was leaning, somehow he’d end up over there with a toolbox. I’ll be driving around and see a guy about his age used to be, out fixing something in the yard, and my mind just goes there.\n\nNobody’s really telling me I’m failing or anything. It’s more me telling myself. Like I should be further along than I am, or I should be handling all this better by now. So… yeah, I guess there’s guilt in that. Or shame. Something like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nIt’s not something I’ve sat down and planned out, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes my mind goes to dark places, especially when I’m driving long stretches and there’s nothing but highway and radio static, and you start thinking too much. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve… thought about ending my life exactly.\n\nI guess I mostly think about being tired. Like wanting everything to let up for a while. But no, I don’t have some plan or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have thoughts about death, even if it's not about yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I see it on the news, like anyone does, and sometimes a song comes on from when I was younger and I’ll think about people who aren’t around anymore… but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI don’t sit around thinking about death all day or anything. Sometimes it kind of passes through my mind, usually when I’m driving past an accident or one of those roadside crosses—you see a lot of odd things from the road, honestly, people leave flowers and little stuffed animals and then months later they’re all faded from the sun.\n\nBut… often? I don’t think so. Not in a way I’ve really kept track of.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything clear like that. I mean, with driving, you’re sitting for so much of the day anyway, so it’s hard to tell what’s me being sluggish and what’s just the job wearing on you.

Work takes a lot out of you, especially driving all day—you see the same roads enough times and everything starts to feel kind of the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience pleasure in any activities?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe sometimes? It’s hard for me to tell what counts as pleasure anymore. Like, if I stop for coffee on my route and the weather’s decent, I can think, “Well, this isn’t bad.” But that’s different than feeling really good about something.\n\nI still do things, you know. I keep busy. And sometimes just being around people at the volunteer place is alright. There’s this one guy there who always tells the same story about his fishing trip, and I’ve heard it probably ten times, but I still let him tell it. Kind of funny how people do that.\n\nBut, yeah… I don’t know. I’m not sure I’d say I feel pleasure the way you mean it. I haven’t really paid close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I really look forward to much in the way people mean, like counting down to it or anything. I still have things I do, same as always. Work, volunteering, errands… sometimes on my route I’ll think about stopping at this one diner if I’m nearby, because they’ve got decent pie, though half the time I forget or I’m running behind.\n\nSo, I mean, maybe little things? But I wouldn’t say I’m excited about them. It’s more like… if they happen, fine. If they don’t, I don’t get too worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. I don’t know if I’d call it sad every minute, exactly. It’s more like this heavy, low feeling that’s just kind of sitting there most days.\n\nI still get through my routes, and I still show up for my volunteer shifts and all that. Like yesterday I was driving past this stretch of road where there’s always construction—same orange barrels for what feels like forever—and I caught myself thinking, “Well, here I am again.” Not really upset, just… flat. Like I’m going through the motions instead of really being in my own life.\n\nSo, yeah. Sad, low… whatever the right word is. It’s been there a long time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about three years now. It kind of started after my father died. Or that’s when I noticed it, anyway.\n\nFunny how time gets away from you after something like that. I still remember clearing out his garage—he kept every little screw and coffee can full of bolts, like he was going to build a whole new house out of leftovers. I thought I was doing alright at the time, just staying busy, but somewhere in there this heaviness just settled in and never really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big way that I’ve noticed. I mean, my belt fits about the same, most of my work pants are the same ones I’ve had for years—though one pair shrank funny in the wash, but that was probably the dryer, not me.\n\nI don’t really weigh myself unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something. Day to day, I haven’t looked in the mirror and thought, “Oh, I’ve really changed.” So… no significant change that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, guilty might not be the exact word, but I do get down on myself a lot.\n\nI keep thinking about my dad and the kind of man he was. He always seemed like he knew what needed doing, just handled things. Even little stuff—like if a neighbor’s fence was leaning, somehow he’d end up over there with a toolbox. I’ll be driving around and see a guy about his age used to be, out fixing something in the yard, and my mind just goes there.\n\nNobody’s really telling me I’m failing or anything. It’s more me telling myself. Like I should be further along than I am, or I should be handling all this better by now. So… yeah, I guess there’s guilt in that. Or shame. Something like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nIt’s not something I’ve sat down and planned out, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes my mind goes to dark places, especially when I’m driving long stretches and there’s nothing but highway and radio static, and you start thinking too much. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve… thought about ending my life exactly.\n\nI guess I mostly think about being tired. Like wanting everything to let up for a while. But no, I don’t have some plan or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have thoughts about death, even if it's not about yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I see it on the news, like anyone does, and sometimes a song comes on from when I was younger and I’ll think about people who aren’t around anymore… but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI don’t sit around thinking about death all day or anything. Sometimes it kind of passes through my mind, usually when I’m driving past an accident or one of those roadside crosses—you see a lot of odd things from the road, honestly, people leave flowers and little stuffed animals and then months later they’re all faded from the sun.\n\nBut… often? I don’t think so. Not in a way I’ve really kept track of.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or sluggish you've been lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything clear like that. I mean, with driving, you’re sitting for so much of the day anyway, so it’s hard to tell what’s me being sluggish and what’s just the job wearing on you.\n\nSome days I move slower getting going, but then once I’m on the route, you kind of just do what needs doing. Deliveries don’t care how you feel, you know? I’ll stop for coffee, stretch my legs at a gas station, talk to whoever’s behind the counter if they’re chatty… it all kind of blurs together.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really kept track of being more active or less active lately.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"intent": "determine presence of anhedonia", "question": "Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?"}

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… I guess I’m here because I’ve just had this low feeling hanging around for a long time. I still do my routes, get through work, and I volunteer some too—been doing that because I don’t like just sitting around—but most days it feels like I’m just… moving through it all. Like I’m there, but not really in my own life, if that makes sense.\n\nI kept thinking it was just being tired, or getting older, you know, long days on the road can do that to a person. But it’s more like this heaviness that doesn’t really lift much.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying the things you used to enjoy, like volunteering?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I still go, so I guess there’s something to it. Volunteering keeps me busy, and I know the folks there, so it’s not like I hate it. But “enjoy” is a funny word, you know? Like when I was younger, I used to get really into things—fixing up my old pickup, going out after work, that kind of stuff.\n\nWith volunteering, I mostly just show up and do what needs doing. Sometimes it feels good to be useful, but I can’t say I’ve really thought about whether I’m enjoying it. It’s more just… part of the routine now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that straight. I mean, I don’t sit around thinking, “Do I like this or not?” I just kind of do things because they’re on the schedule.\n\nLike with volunteering, I show up, help out, talk to whoever’s there. Sometimes it’s fine. I wouldn’t say I’m excited about it, but I don’t know if that means I’ve lost interest or if I’m just… older and more tired. Back when I was younger, sure, I’d get more fired up about stuff, but life was different then. Work takes a lot out of you, especially driving all day—you see the same roads enough times and everything starts to feel kind of the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience pleasure in any activities?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe sometimes? It’s hard for me to tell what counts as pleasure anymore. Like, if I stop for coffee on my route and the weather’s decent, I can think, “Well, this isn’t bad.” But that’s different than feeling really good about something.\n\nI still do things, you know. I keep busy. And sometimes just being around people at the volunteer place is alright. There’s this one guy there who always tells the same story about his fishing trip, and I’ve heard it probably ten times, but I still let him tell it. Kind of funny how people do that.\n\nBut, yeah… I don’t know. I’m not sure I’d say I feel pleasure the way you mean it. I haven’t really paid close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I really look forward to much in the way people mean, like counting down to it or anything. I still have things I do, same as always. Work, volunteering, errands… sometimes on my route I’ll think about stopping at this one diner if I’m nearby, because they’ve got decent pie, though half the time I forget or I’m running behind.\n\nSo, I mean, maybe little things? But I wouldn’t say I’m excited about them. It’s more like… if they happen, fine. If they don’t, I don’t get too worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. I don’t know if I’d call it sad every minute, exactly. It’s more like this heavy, low feeling that’s just kind of sitting there most days.\n\nI still get through my routes, and I still show up for my volunteer shifts and all that. Like yesterday I was driving past this stretch of road where there’s always construction—same orange barrels for what feels like forever—and I caught myself thinking, “Well, here I am again.” Not really upset, just… flat. Like I’m going through the motions instead of really being in my own life.\n\nSo, yeah. Sad, low… whatever the right word is. It’s been there a long time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about three years now. It kind of started after my father died. Or that’s when I noticed it, anyway.\n\nFunny how time gets away from you after something like that. I still remember clearing out his garage—he kept every little screw and coffee can full of bolts, like he was going to build a whole new house out of leftovers. I thought I was doing alright at the time, just staying busy, but somewhere in there this heaviness just settled in and never really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big way that I’ve noticed. I mean, my belt fits about the same, most of my work pants are the same ones I’ve had for years—though one pair shrank funny in the wash, but that was probably the dryer, not me.\n\nI don’t really weigh myself unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something. Day to day, I haven’t looked in the mirror and thought, “Oh, I’ve really changed.” So… no significant change that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, guilty might not be the exact word, but I do get down on myself a lot.\n\nI keep thinking about my dad and the kind of man he was. He always seemed like he knew what needed doing, just handled things. Even little stuff—like if a neighbor’s fence was leaning, somehow he’d end up over there with a toolbox. I’ll be driving around and see a guy about his age used to be, out fixing something in the yard, and my mind just goes there.\n\nNobody’s really telling me I’m failing or anything. It’s more me telling myself. Like I should be further along than I am, or I should be handling all this better by now. So… yeah, I guess there’s guilt in that. Or shame. Something like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nIt’s not something I’ve sat down and planned out, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes my mind goes to dark places, especially when I’m driving long stretches and there’s nothing but highway and radio static, and you start thinking too much. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve… thought about ending my life exactly.\n\nI guess I mostly think about being tired. Like wanting everything to let up for a while. But no, I don’t have some plan or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have thoughts about death, even if it's not about yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I see it on the news, like anyone does, and sometimes a song comes on from when I was younger and I’ll think about people who aren’t around anymore… but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI don’t sit around thinking about death all day or anything. Sometimes it kind of passes through my mind, usually when I’m driving past an accident or one of those roadside crosses—you see a lot of odd things from the road, honestly, people leave flowers and little stuffed animals and then months later they’re all faded from the sun.\n\nBut… often? I don’t think so. Not in a way I’ve really kept track of.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or sluggish you've been lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything clear like that. I mean, with driving, you’re sitting for so much of the day anyway, so it’s hard to tell what’s me being sluggish and what’s just the job wearing on you.\n\nSome days I move slower getting going, but then once I’m on the route, you kind of just do what needs doing. Deliveries don’t care how you feel, you know? I’ll stop for coffee, stretch my legs at a gas station, talk to whoever’s behind the counter if they’re chatty… it all kind of blurs together.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really kept track of being more active or less active lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I guess that’s hard to say.\n\nI still do some of the things I used to do, like I’ll put music on in the truck or watch a game if it’s on somewhere, but whether I’m really enjoying it the same way, I’m not sure. Sometimes it’s just something to pass the time. Like those diners off the interstate that all have the same kind of coffee but somehow the pancakes are different depending on who’s cooking—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m completely unable to enjoy things. I just don’t think I’ve paid close enough attention to know if it’s changed much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"intent": "determine the duration of the patient's current episode of depression", "question": "How long have you been feeling this way without any significant improvement?"}

Like I’m going through the motions instead of really being in my own life.\n\nSo, yeah. Sad, low… whatever the right word is. It’s been there a long time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about three years now. It kind of started after my father died. I still remember clearing out his garage—he kept every little screw and coffee can full of bolts, like he was going to build a whole new house out of leftovers. I thought I was doing alright at the time, just staying busy, but somewhere in there this heaviness just settled in and never really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big way that I’ve noticed. I mean, my belt fits about the same, most of my work pants are the same ones I’ve had for years—though one pair shrank funny in the wash, but that was probably the dryer, not me.\n\nI don’t really weigh myself unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something. Day to day, I haven’t looked in the mirror and thought, “Oh, I’ve really changed.” So… no significant change that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, guilty might not be the exact word, but I do get down on myself a lot.\n\nI keep thinking about my dad and the kind of man he was. He always seemed like he knew what needed doing, just handled things. Even little stuff—like if a neighbor’s fence was leaning, somehow he’d end up over there with a toolbox. I’ll be driving around and see a guy about his age used to be, out fixing something in the yard, and my mind just goes there.\n\nNobody’s really telling me I’m failing or anything. It’s more me telling myself. Like I should be further along than I am, or I should be handling all this better by now. So… yeah, I guess there’s guilt in that. Or shame. I’ll stop for coffee, stretch my legs at a gas station, talk to whoever’s behind the counter if they’re chatty… it all kind of blurs together.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Yeah… I mean, I guess before my father died, I was doing better than I am now. Not like everything was perfect, you know, nobody’s life is all sunshine, especially with work and bills and being on the road all the time. But this… this kind of settled in after he passed.

Day to day, I haven’t looked in the mirror and thought, “Oh, I’ve really changed.” So… no significant change that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, guilty might not be the exact word, but I do get down on myself a lot.\n\nI keep thinking about my dad and the kind of man he was. He always seemed like he knew what needed doing, just handled things. Even little stuff—like if a neighbor’s fence was leaning, somehow he’d end up over there with a toolbox. I’ll be driving around and see a guy about his age used to be, out fixing something in the yard, and my mind just goes there.\n\nNobody’s really telling me I’m failing or anything. It’s more me telling myself. Like I should be further along than I am, or I should be handling all this better by now. So… yeah, I guess there’s guilt in that. Or shame. Something like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nIt’s not something I’ve sat down and planned out, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes my mind goes to dark places, especially when I’m driving long stretches and there’s nothing but highway and radio static, and you start thinking too much. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve… thought about ending my life exactly.\n\nI guess I mostly think about being tired. Like wanting everything to let up for a while. But no, I don’t have some plan or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have thoughts about death, even if it's not about yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I see it on the news, like anyone does, and sometimes a song comes on from when I was younger and I’ll think about people who aren’t around anymore… but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI don’t sit around thinking about death all day or anything. Sometimes it kind of passes through my mind, usually when I’m driving past an accident or one of those roadside crosses—you see a lot of odd things from the road, honestly, people leave flowers and little stuffed animals and then months later they’re all faded from the sun.\n\nBut… often? I don’t think so. Not in a way I’ve really kept track of.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or sluggish you've been lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything clear like that. I mean, with driving, you’re sitting for so much of the day anyway, so it’s hard to tell what’s me being sluggish and what’s just the job wearing on you.\n\nSome days I move slower getting going, but then once I’m on the route, you kind of just do what needs doing. Deliveries don’t care how you feel, you know? I’ll stop for coffee, stretch my legs at a gas station, talk to whoever’s behind the counter if they’re chatty… it all kind of blurs together.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really kept track of being more active or less active lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I guess that’s hard to say.\n\nI still do some of the things I used to do, like I’ll put music on in the truck or watch a game if it’s on somewhere, but whether I’m really enjoying it the same way, I’m not sure. Sometimes it’s just something to pass the time. Like those diners off the interstate that all have the same kind of coffee but somehow the pancakes are different depending on who’s cooking—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m completely unable to enjoy things. I just don’t think I’ve paid close enough attention to know if it’s changed much.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way without any significant improvement?\n\nPatient: I guess… about three years now. It sort of started after my father died.\n\nI don’t know if I noticed it right away, because there was all the usual stuff to deal with—family calls, paperwork, people bringing casseroles like they do, and then everyone goes back to their own lives. But looking back, yeah… I don’t think there’s been any real stretch where it felt like it lifted much since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in your life where you felt significantly better than you do now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess before my father died, I was doing better than I am now. Not like everything was perfect, you know, nobody’s life is all sunshine, especially with work and bills and being on the road all the time. But this… this kind of settled in after he passed.\n\nThat was about three years ago now. Funny how time goes — I’ll still think of something I meant to tell him, like about a route I took or some old truck he would’ve had an opinion about, and then I remember.\n\nSo, yeah… if you mean compared to now, I’d say before that, I probably felt better. I still do my routes, get through work, and I volunteer some too—been doing that because I don’t like just sitting around—but most days it feels like I’m just… moving through it all. Like I’m there, but not really in my own life, if that makes sense.\n\nI kept thinking it was just being tired, or getting older, you know, long days on the road can do that to a person. But it’s more like this heaviness that doesn’t really lift much.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying the things you used to enjoy, like volunteering?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I still go, so I guess there’s something to it. Volunteering keeps me busy, and I know the folks there, so it’s not like I hate it. But “enjoy” is a funny word, you know? Like when I was younger, I used to get really into things—fixing up my old pickup, going out after work, that kind of stuff.\n\nWith volunteering, I mostly just show up and do what needs doing. Sometimes it feels good to be useful, but I can’t say I’ve really thought about whether I’m enjoying it. It’s more just… part of the routine now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that straight. I mean, I don’t sit around thinking, “Do I like this or not?” I just kind of do things because they’re on the schedule.\n\nLike with volunteering, I show up, help out, talk to whoever’s there. Sometimes it’s fine. I wouldn’t say I’m excited about it, but I don’t know if that means I’ve lost interest or if I’m just… older and more tired. Back when I was younger, sure, I’d get more fired up about stuff, but life was different then. Work takes a lot out of you, especially driving all day—you see the same roads enough times and everything starts to feel kind of the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience pleasure in any activities?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe sometimes? It’s hard for me to tell what counts as pleasure anymore. Like, if I stop for coffee on my route and the weather’s decent, I can think, “Well, this isn’t bad.” But that’s different than feeling really good about something.\n\nI still do things, you know. I keep busy. And sometimes just being around people at the volunteer place is alright. There’s this one guy there who always tells the same story about his fishing trip, and I’ve heard it probably ten times, but I still let him tell it. Kind of funny how people do that.\n\nBut, yeah… I don’t know. I’m not sure I’d say I feel pleasure the way you mean it. I haven’t really paid close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I really look forward to much in the way people mean, like counting down to it or anything. I still have things I do, same as always. Work, volunteering, errands… sometimes on my route I’ll think about stopping at this one diner if I’m nearby, because they’ve got decent pie, though half the time I forget or I’m running behind.\n\nSo, I mean, maybe little things? But I wouldn’t say I’m excited about them. It’s more like… if they happen, fine. If they don’t, I don’t get too worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. I don’t know if I’d call it sad every minute, exactly. It’s more like this heavy, low feeling that’s just kind of sitting there most days.\n\nI still get through my routes, and I still show up for my volunteer shifts and all that. Like yesterday I was driving past this stretch of road where there’s always construction—same orange barrels for what feels like forever—and I caught myself thinking, “Well, here I am again.” Not really upset, just… flat. Like I’m going through the motions instead of really being in my own life.\n\nSo, yeah. Sad, low… whatever the right word is. It’s been there a long time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about three years now. It kind of started after my father died. Or that’s when I noticed it, anyway.\n\nFunny how time gets away from you after something like that. I still remember clearing out his garage—he kept every little screw and coffee can full of bolts, like he was going to build a whole new house out of leftovers. I thought I was doing alright at the time, just staying busy, but somewhere in there this heaviness just settled in and never really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big way that I’ve noticed. I mean, my belt fits about the same, most of my work pants are the same ones I’ve had for years—though one pair shrank funny in the wash, but that was probably the dryer, not me.\n\nI don’t really weigh myself unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something. Day to day, I haven’t looked in the mirror and thought, “Oh, I’ve really changed.” So… no significant change that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, guilty might not be the exact word, but I do get down on myself a lot.\n\nI keep thinking about my dad and the kind of man he was. He always seemed like he knew what needed doing, just handled things. Even little stuff—like if a neighbor’s fence was leaning, somehow he’d end up over there with a toolbox. I’ll be driving around and see a guy about his age used to be, out fixing something in the yard, and my mind just goes there.\n\nNobody’s really telling me I’m failing or anything. It’s more me telling myself. Like I should be further along than I am, or I should be handling all this better by now. So… yeah, I guess there’s guilt in that. Or shame. Something like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nIt’s not something I’ve sat down and planned out, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes my mind goes to dark places, especially when I’m driving long stretches and there’s nothing but highway and radio static, and you start thinking too much. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve… thought about ending my life exactly.\n\nI guess I mostly think about being tired. Like wanting everything to let up for a while. But no, I don’t have some plan or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have thoughts about death, even if it's not about yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I see it on the news, like anyone does, and sometimes a song comes on from when I was younger and I’ll think about people who aren’t around anymore… but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI don’t sit around thinking about death all day or anything. Sometimes it kind of passes through my mind, usually when I’m driving past an accident or one of those roadside crosses—you see a lot of odd things from the road, honestly, people leave flowers and little stuffed animals and then months later they’re all faded from the sun.\n\nBut… often? I don’t think so. Not in a way I’ve really kept track of.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or sluggish you've been lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything clear like that. I mean, with driving, you’re sitting for so much of the day anyway, so it’s hard to tell what’s me being sluggish and what’s just the job wearing on you.\n\nSome days I move slower getting going, but then once I’m on the route, you kind of just do what needs doing. Deliveries don’t care how you feel, you know? I’ll stop for coffee, stretch my legs at a gas station, talk to whoever’s behind the counter if they’re chatty… it all kind of blurs together.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really kept track of being more active or less active lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I guess that’s hard to say.\n\nI still do some of the things I used to do, like I’ll put music on in the truck or watch a game if it’s on somewhere, but whether I’m really enjoying it the same way, I’m not sure. Sometimes it’s just something to pass the time. Like those diners off the interstate that all have the same kind of coffee but somehow the pancakes are different depending on who’s cooking—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m completely unable to enjoy things. I just don’t think I’ve paid close enough attention to know if it’s changed much.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way without any significant improvement?\n\nPatient: I guess… about three years now. It sort of started after my father died.\n\nI don’t know if I noticed it right away, because there was all the usual stuff to deal with—family calls, paperwork, people bringing casseroles like they do, and then everyone goes back to their own lives. But looking back, yeah… I don’t think there’s been any real stretch where it felt like it lifted much since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in your life where you felt significantly better than you do now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess before my father died, I was doing better than I am now. Not like everything was perfect, you know, nobody’s life is all sunshine, especially with work and bills and being on the road all the time. But this… this kind of settled in after he passed.\n\nThat was about three years ago now. Funny how time goes — I’ll still think of something I meant to tell him, like about a route I took or some old truck he would’ve had an opinion about, and then I remember.\n\nSo, yeah… if you mean compared to now, I’d say before that, I probably felt better. Since then, I don’t think it’s really let up much.\n\nDoctor: Have you had other periods in your life where you felt this low before your father died?\n\nPatient: Not really like this, no. I mean, I’ve had rough patches before — everybody does, especially when work gets slow or the truck needs repairs at the worst possible time — but this… this has been more steady.\n\nIt’s been about three years, since my father died. That’s when it seemed to start, or at least that’s when I can trace it back to. Before that, I don’t remember being down in this same way for this long.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F32.0", "F32.1", "F33.0", "F33.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

Not like everything was perfect, you know, nobody’s life is all sunshine, especially with work and bills and being on the road all the time. But this… this kind of settled in after he passed.\n\nThat was about three years ago now. Funny how time goes — I’ll still think of something I meant to tell him, like about a route I took or some old truck he would’ve had an opinion about, and then I remember.\n\nSo, yeah… if you mean compared to now, I’d say before that, I probably felt better. Since then, I don’t think it’s really let up much.\n\nDoctor: Have you had other periods in your life where you felt this low before your father died?\n\nPatient: Not really like this, no. I mean, I’ve had rough patches before — everybody does, especially when work gets slow or the truck needs repairs at the worst possible time — but this… this has been more steady.\n\nIt’s been about three years, since my father died. That’s when it seemed to start, or at least that’s when I can trace it back to. Before that, I don’t remember being down in this same way for this long.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever felt this low before your father died, or is this the first time you've experienced such a prolonged period of low feelings?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 50 / driver_sales_worker_or_truck_driver / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about three years, since my father died.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’ve had anything this drawn out before, no. I mean, I’ve had bad spells like anyone — rough weeks, work being a pain, that kind of thing — but this has been going on about three years now, since my father died.

It’s strange, because I can measure it by other things too, like trucks I’ve driven or routes changing, but when I really think about it, it all kind of goes back to then.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F33.0, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… I guess I’m here because I’ve just had this low feeling hanging around for a long time. I still do my routes, get through work, and I volunteer some too—been doing that because I don’t like just sitting around—but most days it feels like I’m just… moving through it all. Like I’m there, but not really in my own life, if that makes sense.\n\nI kept thinking it was just being tired, or getting older, you know, long days on the road can do that to a person. But it’s more like this heaviness that doesn’t really lift much.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying the things you used to enjoy, like volunteering?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I still go, so I guess there’s something to it. Volunteering keeps me busy, and I know the folks there, so it’s not like I hate it. But “enjoy” is a funny word, you know? Like when I was younger, I used to get really into things—fixing up my old pickup, going out after work, that kind of stuff.\n\nWith volunteering, I mostly just show up and do what needs doing. Sometimes it feels good to be useful, but I can’t say I’ve really thought about whether I’m enjoying it. It’s more just… part of the routine now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that straight. I mean, I don’t sit around thinking, “Do I like this or not?” I just kind of do things because they’re on the schedule.\n\nLike with volunteering, I show up, help out, talk to whoever’s there. Sometimes it’s fine. I wouldn’t say I’m excited about it, but I don’t know if that means I’ve lost interest or if I’m just… older and more tired. Back when I was younger, sure, I’d get more fired up about stuff, but life was different then. Work takes a lot out of you, especially driving all day—you see the same roads enough times and everything starts to feel kind of the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience pleasure in any activities?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe sometimes? It’s hard for me to tell what counts as pleasure anymore. Like, if I stop for coffee on my route and the weather’s decent, I can think, “Well, this isn’t bad.” But that’s different than feeling really good about something.\n\nI still do things, you know. I keep busy. And sometimes just being around people at the volunteer place is alright. There’s this one guy there who always tells the same story about his fishing trip, and I’ve heard it probably ten times, but I still let him tell it. Kind of funny how people do that.\n\nBut, yeah… I don’t know. I’m not sure I’d say I feel pleasure the way you mean it. I haven’t really paid close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I really look forward to much in the way people mean, like counting down to it or anything. I still have things I do, same as always. Work, volunteering, errands… sometimes on my route I’ll think about stopping at this one diner if I’m nearby, because they’ve got decent pie, though half the time I forget or I’m running behind.\n\nSo, I mean, maybe little things? But I wouldn’t say I’m excited about them. It’s more like… if they happen, fine. If they don’t, I don’t get too worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. I don’t know if I’d call it sad every minute, exactly. It’s more like this heavy, low feeling that’s just kind of sitting there most days.\n\nI still get through my routes, and I still show up for my volunteer shifts and all that. Like yesterday I was driving past this stretch of road where there’s always construction—same orange barrels for what feels like forever—and I caught myself thinking, “Well, here I am again.” Not really upset, just… flat. Like I’m going through the motions instead of really being in my own life.\n\nSo, yeah. Sad, low… whatever the right word is. It’s been there a long time.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about three years now. It kind of started after my father died. Or that’s when I noticed it, anyway.\n\nFunny how time gets away from you after something like that. I still remember clearing out his garage—he kept every little screw and coffee can full of bolts, like he was going to build a whole new house out of leftovers. I thought I was doing alright at the time, just staying busy, but somewhere in there this heaviness just settled in and never really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big way that I’ve noticed. I mean, my belt fits about the same, most of my work pants are the same ones I’ve had for years—though one pair shrank funny in the wash, but that was probably the dryer, not me.\n\nI don’t really weigh myself unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something. Day to day, I haven’t looked in the mirror and thought, “Oh, I’ve really changed.” So… no significant change that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, guilty might not be the exact word, but I do get down on myself a lot.\n\nI keep thinking about my dad and the kind of man he was. He always seemed like he knew what needed doing, just handled things. Even little stuff—like if a neighbor’s fence was leaning, somehow he’d end up over there with a toolbox. I’ll be driving around and see a guy about his age used to be, out fixing something in the yard, and my mind just goes there.\n\nNobody’s really telling me I’m failing or anything. It’s more me telling myself. Like I should be further along than I am, or I should be handling all this better by now. So… yeah, I guess there’s guilt in that. Or shame. Something like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nIt’s not something I’ve sat down and planned out, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes my mind goes to dark places, especially when I’m driving long stretches and there’s nothing but highway and radio static, and you start thinking too much. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve… thought about ending my life exactly.\n\nI guess I mostly think about being tired. Like wanting everything to let up for a while. But no, I don’t have some plan or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have thoughts about death, even if it's not about yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I see it on the news, like anyone does, and sometimes a song comes on from when I was younger and I’ll think about people who aren’t around anymore… but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI don’t sit around thinking about death all day or anything. Sometimes it kind of passes through my mind, usually when I’m driving past an accident or one of those roadside crosses—you see a lot of odd things from the road, honestly, people leave flowers and little stuffed animals and then months later they’re all faded from the sun.\n\nBut… often? I don’t think so. Not in a way I’ve really kept track of.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or sluggish you've been lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed anything clear like that. I mean, with driving, you’re sitting for so much of the day anyway, so it’s hard to tell what’s me being sluggish and what’s just the job wearing on you.\n\nSome days I move slower getting going, but then once I’m on the route, you kind of just do what needs doing. Deliveries don’t care how you feel, you know? I’ll stop for coffee, stretch my legs at a gas station, talk to whoever’s behind the counter if they’re chatty… it all kind of blurs together.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really kept track of being more active or less active lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I guess that’s hard to say.\n\nI still do some of the things I used to do, like I’ll put music on in the truck or watch a game if it’s on somewhere, but whether I’m really enjoying it the same way, I’m not sure. Sometimes it’s just something to pass the time. Like those diners off the interstate that all have the same kind of coffee but somehow the pancakes are different depending on who’s cooking—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m completely unable to enjoy things. I just don’t think I’ve paid close enough attention to know if it’s changed much.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way without any significant improvement?\n\nPatient: I guess… about three years now. It sort of started after my father died.\n\nI don’t know if I noticed it right away, because there was all the usual stuff to deal with—family calls, paperwork, people bringing casseroles like they do, and then everyone goes back to their own lives. But looking back, yeah… I don’t think there’s been any real stretch where it felt like it lifted much since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in your life where you felt significantly better than you do now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess before my father died, I was doing better than I am now. Not like everything was perfect, you know, nobody’s life is all sunshine, especially with work and bills and being on the road all the time. But this… this kind of settled in after he passed.\n\nThat was about three years ago now. Funny how time goes — I’ll still think of something I meant to tell him, like about a route I took or some old truck he would’ve had an opinion about, and then I remember.\n\nSo, yeah… if you mean compared to now, I’d say before that, I probably felt better. Since then, I don’t think it’s really let up much.\n\nDoctor: Have you had other periods in your life where you felt this low before your father died?\n\nPatient: Not really like this, no. I mean, I’ve had rough patches before — everybody does, especially when work gets slow or the truck needs repairs at the worst possible time — but this… this has been more steady.\n\nIt’s been about three years, since my father died. That’s when it seemed to start, or at least that’s when I can trace it back to. Before that, I don’t remember being down in this same way for this long.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low before your father died, or is this the first time you've experienced such a prolonged period of low feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had anything this drawn out before, no. I mean, I’ve had bad spells like anyone — rough weeks, work being a pain, that kind of thing — but this has been going on about three years now, since my father died.\n\nIt’s strange, because I can measure it by other things too, like trucks I’ve driven or routes changing, but when I really think about it, it all kind of goes back to then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
